<DIV align="center" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 18pt"><U><B>FINANCIAL CONDITION, LIQUIDITY AND CAPITAL RESOURCES</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Net cash generated by operating activities was $84.9&nbsp;million, $72.3&nbsp;million and $136.0&nbsp;million
in the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, 2004 and 2003, respectively. The year-to-year (2005 versus
2004) fluctuation in net cash generated by operating activities primarily reflects increased cash
earnings from operations impacted by the effect of changes in working capital and certain non-cash
items. The 2003 to 2004&nbsp;year-to-year fluctuation in net cash generated by operating activities,
primarily reflect a $74.6&nbsp;million income tax refund received during the second quarter of 2003.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">During the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, 2004 and 2003, cash flows (used in) provided by investing
activities were $(139.6) million, $10.0&nbsp;million and $22.0&nbsp;million, respectively, representing
primarily costs of development and purchases of properties (including the purchase of $78.5&nbsp;million
in 2005 for buildings and land with &#167;1031 funds), offset by proceeds from the disposition of assets
(mostly buildings and land). Proceeds from asset sales were $35.2&nbsp;million, $121.5&nbsp;million and
$124.2&nbsp;million in 2005, 2004 and 2003, respectively.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">During the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, 2004, and 2003, the Company invested approximately $133
million, $80&nbsp;million and $75&nbsp;million, respectively, at Flagler. The increase in 2005 primarily
relates to the purchase of two office buildings in the first quarter 2005 by Flagler and FECR using
&#167;1031 proceeds, totaling $60.6&nbsp;million. Flagler&#146;s 2005 capital expenditures reflect a total of $76
million for acquisitions, $48&nbsp;million for new development and $9&nbsp;million for improvements to
current buildings.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Capital investment at Flagler for 2006 is expected to be between $80&nbsp;million and $90&nbsp;million, for
new development ($73&nbsp;million to $78&nbsp;million) and improvements to current buildings ($7&nbsp;million to
$12&nbsp;million) and before consideration for the Codina Acquisition.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">For the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, 2004 and 2003, the Company&#146;s capital investments at FECR
were approximately $39&nbsp;million, $30&nbsp;million and $27&nbsp;million, respectively. Capital investment at
FECR for 2006 is expected to be between $48&nbsp;million and $53&nbsp;million, for normative improvements
($28&nbsp;million to $31&nbsp;million) and productivity/growth improvements ($20&nbsp;million to $22&nbsp;million) and
before the purchase of any strategic land parcels to be inventoried for future Railway customers.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Net cash provided by (used in) financing activities was $4.5&nbsp;million, $(81.1) million and $(116.9)
million in the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, 2004 and 2003, respectively. Financing activities
for 2005 included payments for the purchase of common stock, increased mortgage debt payments at
Flagler, and increased dividend payments, offset by proceeds from the exercise of stock options.
2004 cash (used in) financing activities includes proceeds from the issuance of $105&nbsp;million
seven-year non-recourse mortgage notes issued in the third quarter of 2004 offset by the purchase
of 5.5&nbsp;million shares of common stock from The Alfred I. duPont Testamentary Trust and The Nemours
Foundation for approximately $191&nbsp;million. Finance activities in 2003 were primarily the repayment
($53&nbsp;million) of monies borrowed on the line of credit and the payment ($60.5&nbsp;million) of dividends
including a special dividend of $1.50 per share.
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">21

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">At December&nbsp;31, 2005, the standby letters of credit were primarily for construction
commitments related to building and infrastructure projects at Flagler&#146;s project locations and to
cover deductibles related to insurance policies. Other commercial commitments primarily include
amounts committed to customers and vendors for construction projects at Flagler-owned developments.

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">On January&nbsp;6, 2006, the Company announced that a newly formed holding company and certain Company
subsidiaries had entered into various agreements to acquire privately held Codina Group, Inc. and
certain related property interests held directly or indirectly by Mr.&nbsp;Armando Codina for total
consideration of $270&nbsp;million. See earlier acquisition discussion contained in this item of the
form 10-K.

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">On February&nbsp;22, 2005, the Company entered into a new unsecured $150&nbsp;million, 5-year revolving
credit agreement with a syndication of banks. The new credit facility provides a more favorable
interest rate and terms and replaces the Company&#146;s previous $200&nbsp;million credit agreement. The
Company pays (quarterly)&nbsp;commitment fees, as applicable under the agreement, at a range of 12.5 to
25.0 basis points. The Company&#146;s revolving credit agreement requires the maintenance of certain
financial ratios (interest coverage and leverage) and maintenance of minimum established levels of
net worth. The Company believes the most restrictive of such ratios is the Leverage Ratio (as
defined in the Credit Facility Agreement). The Credit Facility Agreement provides that at December
31, 2005, the Company&#146;s Adjusted Total Debt/Adjusted Total EBITDA ratio shall be no greater than
3.00 to 1.00. At December&nbsp;31, 2005, the Company&#146;s actual Adjusted Total Debt/Adjusted Total EBITDA
ratio was 0.02 to 1.00. In addition there are various established limitations (dollars and
otherwise) on certain types of liens, investments (including loans and advances) and acquisitions,
limitation on transactions with affiliates, merger and sales of all or substantially all of the
assets; and use of proceeds. Some of the above covenants provide specific exclusion of certain
financing
</DIV>

</DIV>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">and investing activities at Flagler. Borrowings under the credit agreement bear interest
at variable rates linked to the LIBOR Index. Interest on borrowings is due and payable on the
&#147;rollover date&#148; for each draw. Outstanding borrowings can be paid at any time by the borrower, or
at the conclusion of the facility&#146;s term. At December&nbsp;31, 2005, there were no borrowings
outstanding on this facility.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">On January&nbsp;6, 2006, the Company announced that a newly formed holding company and certain Company
subsidiaries had entered into various agreements to acquire privately held Codina Group, Inc. and
certain related property interests held directly or indirectly by Mr.&nbsp;Armando Codina for total
consideration of $270&nbsp;million. The total consideration of $270&nbsp;million includes $66&nbsp;million of
cash for certain property interests, assumption or repayment of $36&nbsp;million of debt and up to $168
million of equity securities (includes $85&nbsp;million of contingent consideration). The contingent
equity is contingent upon achievement of certain value based milestones including $30&nbsp;million tied
to certain land use approvals, $30&nbsp;million upon certain partnership distributions from a joint
venture, $20&nbsp;million is based on cumulative FECI stock price improvements of up to an additional
$27.00 over the next five years; and $5&nbsp;million is dependent on a decision to move forward with a
proposed residential development. The purchase price is subject to post-closing adjustment based
on certain closing conditions. The Codina acquisition, subject to certain closing conditions, is
expected to close during the first quarter of 2006. The Company expects to use existing cash and
cash equivalents and potential borrowings under its un-drawn $150&nbsp;million revolving credit
agreement (subject to an amendment for such acquisition) to satisfy the cash and debt repayment
needs of closing the acquisition.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The properties being acquired are expected to be developed into approximately 11&nbsp;million sq. ft. of
industrial space, 600,000 sq. ft. of office space, 750,000 sq. ft. of retail space and 3,500
multi-family units. The net investment to fully develop these properties, after out parcel and
condo sales, is nearly $750&nbsp;million.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B><I>Impact from Hurricanes</I></B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The Railway suspended service early Sunday, October&nbsp;23, 2005, in anticipation of the arrival of
Hurricane Wilma. Rail service was disrupted for approximately two weeks due to widespread power
outages that prevented automatic grade crossing warning systems and railroad signals from
operating. All scheduled rail service resumed on November&nbsp;7, 2005 after all sections of track
power was restored and all rail crossings passed inspection.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The Company estimates that FECR will incur approximately $3.4&nbsp;million of incremental costs related
to clean-up costs, property damage, grade crossing operations, support and additional train crew
costs as a result of trains operating at reduced speeds. The Railway is in the process of filing
claims for reimbursements and recoveries; however, the ultimate total amount of these recoveries
cannot be estimated at this time. Included in this amount is $0.5&nbsp;million in costs related to
Hurricanes Katrina and Rita.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Several of Flagler&#146;s buildings located in Flagler Station sustained damage from Hurricane Wilma,
primarily roofs and landscaping. The total damage (cash outlays) is estimated to be approximately
$7.0&nbsp;million to $7.5&nbsp;million, of which approximately $4.0&nbsp;million to $5.0&nbsp;million is currently
expected to be recovered from insurance and tenants. There was additional damage due to Hurricane
Katrina that was less than $0.1&nbsp;million.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The American Institute of CPAs (AICPA)&nbsp;issued Technical Practice Aide (TPA) &#167;5400.05 in September
2005, which provides guidance on the accounting treatment of natural disaster related costs. TPA
&#167;5400.05 does not allow recognition of a natural disaster related cost until such time that the
cost is a legal obligation of the Company. Accordingly, the Company has recorded those costs where
a legal obligation was present at December&nbsp;31, 2005. However, in an effort to provide a more
complete accounting for the cost of the hurricane damages management provides the table below.
This table reflects the total estimated cost and expected recoveries related to the hurricane
damage. The table also shows costs and recoveries reflected on the operating results and capital
accounts for the fourth quarter and full years of 2005 and 2004.
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">24
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B><I>Railway</I></B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">FECR&#146;s management team continued its focus on improving profitability during 2005 and 2004.
Continuing investments in both operational and human capital were made during these periods to
enhance customer service and safety while controlling costs. FECR transported record levels of
aggregate during 2005 and 2004 in support of the Florida construction industry. The intermodal
business continues to be strong as is evidenced by its tenth consecutive quarter over quarter
growth. Leading the way is the growth of our local intermodal business with increased business in
our international, retail and parcel LTL segments. The &#147;Hurricane Train&#148; service, an Atlanta based
intermodal service introduced in 2001 also contributed to the increased revenues. Also critical to
our growth was the complete re-branding and integration of FECR&#146;s drayage services into our
intermodal product mix. During 2005 and 2004, the Company reinvested approximately $39&nbsp;million and
$30&nbsp;million, respectively, of capital in equipment, land, improved physical plant and new
technologies at FECR.
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">25
</DIV>

</TABLE>
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Revenues &#151; 2005 versus 2004</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Railway segment revenues increased $37.1&nbsp;million to $237.9&nbsp;million in 2005 from $200.8&nbsp;million in
2004. Carload revenues increased $18.6&nbsp;million (Rate/Length of Haul &#151; $7.3&nbsp;million; Volume &#151; $11.3
million) over 2004 levels, which was driven by record levels of traffic. Aggregate revenue
increased $9.6&nbsp;million in 2005 over 2004 mainly due to the continued demand in the Florida
construction market. Foodstuffs and Kindred products increased $2.1&nbsp;million due to growth in the
existing customer base as well as the addition of a new customer. Hurricane relief products
continued to be shipped during the year 2005 as evidenced by the increased revenues ($1.5&nbsp;million
and $1.9&nbsp;million) in both Construction Materials and Paper and Lumber commodities, respectively.
Vehicles increased $1.4&nbsp;million mainly due to volume. Intermodal revenues increased $16.6&nbsp;million
(Rate &#151; $8.7&nbsp;million; Volume &#151; $7.9&nbsp;million) in 2005 over 2004. Leading this increase was the
local intermodal business and dray services. The local intermodal business growth was mainly
attributed to retail customers, international shippers and parcel LTL carriers. Included in
revenues for the years ending December&nbsp;31, 2005 and 2004, respectively, is $13.2&nbsp;million and $4.6
million of fuel surcharges for increased fuel prices. The variance in fuel surcharges year over
year of $8.6&nbsp;million is included in the Rate/Length of Haul variance above. The Company changed
its fuel policy effective September&nbsp;1, 2005 to more accurately match increasing fuel prices to fuel
surcharges passed on to our customers. The year to date impact of the policy change is estimated
at $0.8&nbsp;million in additional fuel surcharge revenue. FECR incurred one day with no traffic and
thirteen days of limited traffic in fourth quarter 2005 due to Hurricane Wilma.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Revenues &#151; 2004 versus 2003</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Railway segment&#146;s operating revenues increased $19.7&nbsp;million to $200.8&nbsp;million in 2004 versus
$181.1&nbsp;million in 2003. Most of the increase resulted from intermodal revenues ($13.7&nbsp;million) and
aggregate rock revenues (crushed stone) ($4.8&nbsp;million). Freight revenues were $197.1&nbsp;million for
2004, compared with $177.8&nbsp;million for 2003. Intermodal revenues increased $13.7&nbsp;million to $86.4
million for 2004. After years of decline from haulage traffic with a connecting carrier, this
revenue increased $2.2&nbsp;million to $9.9&nbsp;million in 2004. The &#147;Hurricane Train&#148; service from Atlanta
resulted in additional revenues of $3.2&nbsp;million, showing its success in the Atlanta marketplace,
and local traffic (including drayage) increased $9.0&nbsp;million over 2003 primarily from increased
traffic from the retail segment, motor carriers, parcel LTL carriers and the international segment.
Offsetting these intermodal revenue increases was a reduction in the intermodal traffic from one
of its interline carriers. Freight revenues from aggregate rock/crushed stone traffic increased
$4.8&nbsp;million in 2004 due to an increase in construction demand throughout the state of Florida.
Revenues from construction materials increased $0.7&nbsp;million due to the high demand for domestic
building products as Florida&#146;s construction and re-construction (hurricane related) demand
increased. Revenues from foodstuff traffic increased $1.0&nbsp;million due to increased business from
one major customer and new customers locating on FECR&#146;s line. Automotive (vehicles)&nbsp;traffic
decreased $0.6&nbsp;million to $17.6&nbsp;million primarily due to the change in shipment patterns for
automobiles bought back from rental car agencies.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Expenses &#151; 2005 versus 2004</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Operating expenses increased $20.7&nbsp;million to $174.1&nbsp;million for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2005,
compared with $153.4&nbsp;million for the same period last year. Compensation and benefits increased
$3.9&nbsp;million due to general wage increases, and variable compensation expenses (stock compensation
costs and bonus). Fuel expense increased $8.8&nbsp;million of which $7.2&nbsp;million is related to price
increases. Equipment rents increased $1.0&nbsp;million due to increased rates and volume. Car hire
increased $0.9&nbsp;million due to volume and rate increases as well as reductions in off line rents for
equipment that is at the end of its life cycle. Increased 2005 depreciation expenses ($1.7
million) results primarily from $0.8&nbsp;million of depreciation on 2005 capital additions and $0.7
million of increased depreciation resulting from the adjusted depreciation lives from the
locomotive and rolling stock economic engineering studies. Load/unload expenses increased $1.2
million primarily due to volume. Materials increased $2.3&nbsp;million primarily due to freight car
wheel replacement and repairs required by rules of the Association of American Railroads, the
Company raising its own repair standards for owned equipment, the replacement of certain defective
wheels manufactured in 2005 by a certain vendor, and the increased price of steel. Outside
contractor delivery costs increased $2.7&nbsp;million due to increased business and increased costs for
driver services. The above expense increases were offset by a decrease in Casualty &#038; Insurance of
$0.9&nbsp;million primarily due to lower personal injury claims. The decrease in other expenses of $0.3&nbsp;million
is mainly attributed to $2.0&nbsp;million in net costs incurred in 2005 for Hurricanes Wilma, Katrina
and Rita compared to net costs incurred in 2004 for an $0.8&nbsp;million main line derailment and $2.0
million for Hurricanes Frances and Jeanne.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">During 2003, FECR commenced an &#147;engineering and economics&#148; based study of its principal
right-of-way and equipment assets to develop a multi-year maintenance and replacement plan and
associated estimates of the assets&#146; remaining lives. The engineering studies segregated the
equipment and right-of-way assets into the following major groupings: locomotives, rolling stock
and equipment (e.g., hoppers, flatcars, auto racks, trailers, etc.), bridges, and track structure
(rail, ties, ballast, and other track material). During the third and fourth quarters of 2004, the
engineering studies (including associated estimated
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">27
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">depreciation lives) for locomotives and rolling stock were completed. During the first quarter of
2005, the engineering study for bridges was finalized. The track structure engineering study was
finalized in the fourth quarter of 2005.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The track study evaluated FECR&#146;s entire mainline, branch lines and yards from Jacksonville to
Miami, Florida. The study&#146;s fieldwork involved hi-rail inspections of the main line, tie samples
in each mile of track and rail measurements taken at each tie sample location. In each yard,
samples of tie and rail conditions were taken to develop a general picture of the overall condition
of the yard.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">From the track study, FECR identified four major components for its track assets: rail, ties,
ballast and other track material (OTM). Overall, the study indicated the track system to be in
good condition. The study generally recommends that we continue our general maintenance programs
(e.g., surfacing, bolt fastening, undercutting, etc.) at our current levels. In 2005, $13.8
million was spent on our track maintenance programs. The 2006 budget includes $13.7&nbsp;million of
track maintenance programs, which is in line with the study recommendations. Nothing significant
was found in yard maintenance during the track study.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The study also evaluated the condition and replacement of our track assets. One of the major
components that impact the replacement of rail and track materials is tonnage. The Transportation
Test Center (TTCI)&nbsp;in Pueblo, Colorado indicates a tangent rail life expectancy based on 1.460
billion gross tons for generic mainline rail with a six-inch base (132, 136 and 141 pound).
Between the periods of 1970 &#150; 1977, FECR replaced approximately 74% of its main line track with
generally small amounts of rail replacement from 1978 through 2005. The average annual tonnage on
FECR&#146;s rail averaged approximately 9&nbsp;million gross tons during the 1970&#146;s. Subsequent to the
1970&#146;s, annual tonnages have steadily increased with annual tonnage during the last decade of
approximately 37&nbsp;million gross tons. Given these historical tonnage amounts, FECR expects our
existing rail assets to meet their current life expectancy of 50&nbsp;years with no impact on
depreciation rates. Going forward, if FECR experiences expected annual tonnage levels of 50
million gross tons, the lives for newly installed rail and other track components after December
31, 2005 will need to be adjusted potentially within a range of 35 to 45&nbsp;years. The potential
annual depreciation impact for the change in rail and OTM assets (if adjusted to a range of 35 &#150; 45
years) would be less than $0.1&nbsp;million in 2006 (year one of change) and range from $0.2&nbsp;million to
$0.4&nbsp;million in year 2015 (year 10 after change) based on normative expected capital budgets. The
study indicated no substantive change in the depreciable lives of ties and ballast, at generally 40
years.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The bridge study covered FECR&#146;s 72 bridges or approximately 13,000 linear feet of bridge assets
(30% were inspected during the study). The bridge study indicated no substantive change in the
depreciable lives and generally, bridges would continue to be depreciated over a 61.5-year life.
The bridge study also indicated that while FECR&#146;s bridge assets are in acceptable operational
condition based on the Company&#146;s continuing maintenance and inspection programs, FECR is entering a
10 to 20&nbsp;year period where, based on the age and construction materials used, certain bridges will
be studied further, monitored and possibly replaced. The management team does not expect
replacement costs for bridges to materially alter its normative annual capital budget, however this
estimate is subject to change, and such changes could be material. The study did not include the
downtown Jacksonville bridge, which spans the St. Johns River, which was evaluated separately (due
to its scale and design). The study recommended rehabilitation of the pier protection structures
for the Jacksonville bridge main channel. This work commenced in 2005 and will be completed in the
first quarter 2006. No other significant change in capital maintenance or replacement programs was
noted.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The locomotive and rolling stock study indicated no substantive change in the annual maintenance or
capital replacement programs. FECR will continue its general practice of maintaining locomotives
and rolling stock for the entirety of their useful operating life.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">From the locomotive engineering study, FECR identified three major sub-components of its locomotive
assets: Hulk/Chassis, Locomotive engine and Combos (e.g., wheel assemblies, electric motors, etc.)
The replacement and resulting depreciable lives for each of these sub-components is 50&nbsp;years, 7
years and 3&nbsp;years, respectively. Annual depreciation is expected to increase approximately 11% or
$0.3&nbsp;million as a result of the study. These depreciable lives were implemented in the fourth
quarter of 2004.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">From the rolling stock engineering study, FECR identified three major sub-components of its rolling
stock assets: Hulk/Chassis, wheel assemblies (e.g., trucks), and doors. The replacement and
resulting depreciable lives for each of these sub-components is 40 to 50&nbsp;years, 7&nbsp;years and 7
years, respectively. General FECR equipment, which was included in the rolling stock study, has
depreciable lives of 10 to 20&nbsp;years. Annual depreciation is expected to increase approximately 11%
or $0.4&nbsp;million as a result of the study. These depreciable lives were implemented in the first
quarter of 2005.
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">28
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">These studies, their conclusions and on-going evaluations may result in material changes in FECR&#146;s
maintenance and capital spending and in the estimated remaining useful lives of its property, plant
and equipment, which may affect future depreciation rates and expense.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Expenses &#151; 2004 versus 2003</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Operating expenses increased $15.3&nbsp;million to $153.4&nbsp;million in 2004, compared with $138.1
million in 2003. A significant portion of the increased expenses resulted from increased volumes
and the impact of three Florida hurricanes that impacted the property. Compensation and benefits
increased $3.6&nbsp;million or 7.0% to $54.8&nbsp;million in 2004 primarily because of the Railway&#146;s
general wage increases for 2004, additional labor costs to move higher traffic levels and
increases in variable compensation. Fuel expenses increased $2.6&nbsp;million to $16.3&nbsp;million due to
increased fuel price ($2.4&nbsp;million) and consumption ($0.4&nbsp;million) and a reduction due to
non-locomotive fuel ($0.2&nbsp;million). Depreciation expenses increased $0.6&nbsp;million in 2004 due to
increased levels of 2003 and 2004 capital expenditures. Purchased services increased $1.2
million and materials increased $1.1&nbsp;million due to additional repairs and services to freight
cars and trailers due to additional business. Outside contractor delivery costs increased $3.6
million during 2004 due to increased volumes of business. Hurricanes Charley, Frances and Jeanne
disrupted Railway operations in the third quarter and early fourth quarter 2004. Clean up and
repair costs were $3.0&nbsp;million primarily for tree removal, signal repairs, crossing repairs,
increased train operations costs and building damages. Approximately $1.0&nbsp;million of these costs
were reimbursed by various governmental agencies. Net hurricane costs are included in other
expenses.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B><I>Real Estate</I></B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">During 2005, Flagler added two buildings to its portfolio of income-producing properties through
its development activities and two buildings through acquisition. At December&nbsp;31, 2005, an
additional five buildings were in lease up. The Company began construction of one additional
building with completion expected in 2006. The Company expects to start construction on five to
seven additional new buildings during 2006 due to increased demand and improved market conditions.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">At December&nbsp;31, 2005 Flagler&#146;s existing land holdings, with entitlements for construction of 7.3
million sq. ft. of office space, 2.2&nbsp;million sq. ft. of industrial space and 0.6&nbsp;million sq. ft. of
retail space, 500 multi-family units, and 380 hotel rooms, offers opportunities for further
development or sale. The Company intends to continue its program to realize the value of its
extensive land holdings at Flagler and the Railway by developing or monetizing them. Flagler&#146;s
future rate of growth, profitability, capital investment and sales of land depends, in part, on the
extent of future demand in Florida&#146;s commercial real estate markets, operating costs, and rental
rates for leases, which are subject to uncertainty.
</DIV>


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">29
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><I>Same Store </I>includes buildings where a comparison of operating results from the prior
year to the current year is meaningful, as these buildings were owned and had stabilized
occupancy and operating expenses as of the beginning of the prior year. For the year
2005, Same Store buildings had stabilized occupancy and operating expenses as of January
1, 2004. A building is considered to have stabilized occupancy at the earlier of (i)
attainment of 90% physical occupancy or (ii)&nbsp;the one-year anniversary of certificate of
occupancy issuance.

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><U> </U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><I>Acquired in 2005 </I>are buildings that were acquired during the year. These buildings do not have a
full year of comparative operating results.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Revenues
from Continuing Operations &#151; 2005 versus 2004</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Flagler rental and services revenues increased $17.8&nbsp;million or 25.5% from $69.6&nbsp;million for the
twelve months ended December&nbsp;31, 2004 to $87.4&nbsp;million for the twelve months ended December&nbsp;31,
2005. Operating properties revenue in 2005 increased $17.2&nbsp;million compared to 2004. Same Store
occupancy remained the same at 95% at December&nbsp;31, 2005 compared to December&nbsp;31, 2004. Same Store
revenues increased $4.2&nbsp;million compared to the prior year, mainly attributable to occupancy
improvements. Properties beginning operations in 2004 contributed revenue of $4.7&nbsp;million during
2005 for an increase of $3.1&nbsp;million over 2004 levels. This increase represents successful leasing
activity and improved occupancy at these buildings. Properties developed in 2005 generated
revenues of $4.2&nbsp;million in 2005 and properties acquired in 2005 contributed $5.7&nbsp;million in
revenues. Other rental and services revenues included in Land and Corporate Overhead increased
$0.6&nbsp;million primarily related to commissions earned on third party transactions.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">For all leases renewed during calendar 2005, beginning cash rental rates averaged approximately 5%
less than the ending rate of the expiring leases, however straight-line rental rates over the new
lease term averaged approximately 1% higher than the average straight-line rental rates of the
expiring leases.
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">33
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Flagler held 64 finished buildings with 7.4&nbsp;million sq. ft. and occupancy of 95% at December&nbsp;31,
2005. Same Store properties include 6.4&nbsp;million sq. ft. and 95% occupancy at December&nbsp;31, 2005
compared to 95% at December&nbsp;31, 2004.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Flagler sales revenue decreased $74.9&nbsp;million from $99.7&nbsp;million in 2004 to $24.8&nbsp;million in 2005
due to fewer land sales in 2005.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Revenues
from Continuing Operations &#151; 2004 versus 2003</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Flagler rental and services revenues increased $6.0&nbsp;million or 9.4% from $63.6&nbsp;million for the
twelve months ended December&nbsp;31, 2003 to $69.6&nbsp;million for the twelve months ended December&nbsp;31,
2004. Operating properties revenue in 2004 increased $6.1&nbsp;million compared to 2003. Same store
occupancy increased to 94% at December&nbsp;31, 2004 compared to occupancy of 87% at December&nbsp;31, 2003.
Same store revenues increased $4.2&nbsp;million compared to the prior year, mainly due to occupancy
improvements and the 2004 full year impact of properties acquired during 2003. Income from land
rents decreased $0.2&nbsp;million due to the sale of certain land parcels. Income from equity
investments decreased $0.1&nbsp;million due to the dissolution of Flagler&#146;s partnership interests in
2003.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">For all leases renewed during calendar 2004, beginning cash rental rates averaged approximately 3%
less than the ending rate of the expiring leases, however straight-line rental rates over the new
lease term averaged approximately 3% higher than the average straight-line rental rates of the
expiring leases.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Flagler held 60 finished buildings with 6.6&nbsp;million sq. ft. and occupancy of 95% at December&nbsp;31,
2004.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Flagler sales revenue increased from $63.5&nbsp;million in 2003 to $99.7&nbsp;million in 2004 related
primarily to the $80&nbsp;million sale of a 460-acre parcel in Doral, Florida in December&nbsp;2004.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Expenses
from Continuing Operations &#151; 2005 versus 2004</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Flagler&#146;s rental and services expenses increased $12.5&nbsp;million or 20.7% from $60.6&nbsp;million for the
twelve months 2004 to $73.1&nbsp;million for the twelve months 2005. Included in these amounts are
increases in depreciation and amortization of $5.7&nbsp;million due primarily to the addition of new
properties in 2005 and a property beginning operations at the end of 2004. Expenses related to
operating properties increased by $11.2&nbsp;million from $47.0&nbsp;million in 2004 to $58.2&nbsp;million in
2005. Same Store operating properties expenses increased by $1.2&nbsp;million. This increase in Same
Store expense is composed of a $1.1&nbsp;million increase of mainly variable expenses related to
increased average occupancy, $0.3&nbsp;million increase related to real estate taxes, a $0.8&nbsp;million
increase related to the impact of Hurricanes Wilma and Katrina, and a $1.0&nbsp;million decrease in
depreciation and amortization. The decrease in depreciation expense is associated with a write off
of $1.5&nbsp;million of tenant improvements during the first quarter 2004 related to the remodeling of
duPont Center in Jacksonville, Florida. Properties beginning operations in 2004 contributed an
increase in expenses of $2.0&nbsp;million during the twelve months in 2005. Properties developed in
2005 contributed $2.2&nbsp;million in expenses and properties acquired in 2005 had expenses of $6.0
million.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Land holdings and corporate overhead expenses increased by $1.3&nbsp;million compared to prior year.
Real estate taxes decreased $1.2&nbsp;million on owned and acquired land due to sales of property,
primarily Section&nbsp;8 in Flagler Station (December&nbsp;2004). Additionally, land holdings and corporate
overhead had a $1.8&nbsp;million increase in Salaries, General &#038; Administrative, primarily 2005
severance and other payroll related costs, and a $0.7&nbsp;million increase in depreciation expense.
Depreciation expense includes depreciation expense on infrastructure, including the interchange
asset, partially offset by a reimbursement from the City of Jacksonville. The total reimbursement
received in June&nbsp;2005 from the City of Jacksonville was $0.7&nbsp;million, of which $0.5&nbsp;million was
applied against depreciation expense on infrastructure assets. The remaining $0.2&nbsp;million of the
reimbursement was recorded as other non-operating income.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Flagler&#146;s cost of land sales (i.e., mostly basis) decreased $7.9&nbsp;million due to decreased sale
activity, from $14.9&nbsp;million for the twelve months ended December&nbsp;31, 2004 to $7.0&nbsp;million for the
twelve months ended December&nbsp;31, 2005.
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">34
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Expenses
from Continuing Operations &#151; 2004 versus 2003</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Flagler&#146;s operating expenses increased $4.7&nbsp;million or 8.4% from $55.9&nbsp;million in 2003 to $60.6
million in 2004. Properties beginning operations in 2004 contributed $1.1&nbsp;million of the increase,
including $0.6&nbsp;million of depreciation and amortization. Same store operating expenses increased
$1.0&nbsp;million related to the 2004 full year impact of properties acquired during 2003 ($0.3
million), increased owners&#146; association costs ($0.4&nbsp;million), increased repairs and maintenance
($0.1&nbsp;million), increased janitorial costs ($0.1&nbsp;million), increased electrical costs related to
occupancy increases ($0.1&nbsp;million), increased management and administrative costs ($0.2&nbsp;million)
and other expense increases ($0.1&nbsp;million). These cost increases were offset by decreased
landscaping costs ($0.2&nbsp;million) and decreased insurance cost ($0.1&nbsp;million). Land holding costs
increased $2.9&nbsp;million primarily related to increased real estate taxes ($1.0&nbsp;million) and
increased depreciation ($1.9&nbsp;million) associated with unallocated infrastructure placed in service
during 2004. These increased operating expenses were offset by decreased corporate overhead of
$0.3&nbsp;million.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Flagler&#146;s cost of land sales decreased from $33.9&nbsp;million to $14.9&nbsp;million, a decrease of $19.0
million or 56.2%.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Results from Discontinued Operations</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">In accordance with SFAS 144, &#147;Accounting for the Impairment or Disposal of Long-Lived Assets&#148; (SFAS
144), components of Flagler that meet certain criteria have been accounted for as discontinued
operations. Therefore, income or loss attributable to the operations and sale of the components
classified as discontinued operations are presented in the summary of operating results as
discontinued operations, net of applicable income taxes.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">During March&nbsp;2004, Flagler sold a 60,000-sq. ft. build-to-suit office building for $12.6&nbsp;million
recognizing a pre-tax gain of $3.7&nbsp;million, or $2.3&nbsp;million, net of applicable taxes. During
September&nbsp;2004, Flagler sold a 147,000-sq. ft. industrial building for $4.1&nbsp;million, for a $0.4
million pre-tax gain, or $0.3&nbsp;million, net of applicable taxes.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Discontinued operations include the operations of the buildings sold in 2004, the operations of an
industrial building sold in November&nbsp;2003, and Flagler&#146;s 50% interest in three buildings formerly
held in partnership with Duke Realty sold during July&nbsp;2003. The operating results of these
properties, as well as the gains on the sales of these properties are included in discontinued
operations for all periods presented.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Corporate Expenses</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Corporate expenses for the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2005 and 2004 were $22.4&nbsp;million and $15.1
million, respectively. Increases primarily relate to costs associated with the appointment of Mr.
Adolfo Henriques as Chairman, President, and Chief Executive Officer and termination benefits
provided to Mr.&nbsp;Robert W. Anestis. These expenses totaled $9.1&nbsp;million in the first quarter and
included $5.3&nbsp;million of additional equity compensation ($1.9&nbsp;million due to the accelerated
vesting of equity compensation), $3.3&nbsp;million of severance payments and $0.5&nbsp;million of other
benefits.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Corporate expenses increased from $13.1&nbsp;million in 2003 to $15.1&nbsp;million in 2004. Increases during
2004 primarily relate to costs associated with increased incentive-based compensation (i.e.,
restricted stock and annual bonuses) expense. In a first quarter 2004 transaction (the
&#147;Transaction&#148;) initiated at the request of the Board of Directors, the Company cancelled vested,
in-the-money stock options (the &#147;Options&#148;) to acquire 144,000 shares of the Company&#146;s common stock
held by the Company&#146;s former Chief Executive Officer (&#147;CEO&#148;), Robert W. Anestis. The embedded
value of the Options (fair market value less strike price) was $2&nbsp;million. The embedded value,
less applicable tax withholding, was paid in common stock. The CEO was issued 36,407 shares
(valued at $35.06, which was the closing price on February&nbsp;27, 2004). The stock, together with
applicable withholdings, represents the pre-tax embedded value. Because the original stock option
award was cancelled and the Company
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">35
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">replaced this award with FECI common stock, in accordance with FASB Interpretation No.&nbsp;44, the
Company was required to recognize compensation expense in the amount of $2&nbsp;million with respect to
the Transaction.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Interest Expense</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Interest expense for the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2005 and 2004 was $20.1&nbsp;million and $16.7
million, respectively. Interest cost (i.e., cash paid for interest) was $22.3&nbsp;million and $19.3
million for 2005 and 2004, respectively. The increased interest expense, and cash paid for
interest resulted primarily from the issuance of additional mortgage notes in August&nbsp;2004 at
Flagler.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Interest expense for the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2004 and 2003 was $16.7&nbsp;million and $17.1
million, respectively. The lower interest expense resulted primarily from additional interest
being capitalized into new construction during 2004. Interest cost (i.e., cash paid for interest)
was $19.3&nbsp;million and $18.0&nbsp;million for 2004 and 2003, respectively. Cash paid for interest
increased primarily due to increased levels of mortgage notes at Flagler.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Other Income</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Other income for 2005 and 2004 was $14.6&nbsp;million and $13.0&nbsp;million, respectively. Pipe and wire
crossing income for 2004 included $2.6&nbsp;million from two one-time wire crossing agreements entered
into in 2004. Included in 2005 results is income of $3.0&nbsp;million associated with the sale of air
rights, and related surface and sub-surface easements and licenses on the Railway&#146;s right-of-way.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Other income for 2004 and 2003 was $13.0&nbsp;million and $9.5&nbsp;million, respectively. Pipe and wire
crossing income for 2004 included $2.6&nbsp;million from two one-time wire crossing agreements entered
into in 2004.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Income Tax</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Income tax expenses represent an effective rate of 37.0% in 2005, compared to 39.0 % in 2004 and
38.5% in 2003. 2005&#146;s decrease in effective rate reflects newly enacted tax credits related to
track maintenance at FECR offset by certain non-deductible section 162(m) compensation over $1.0
million for Mr.&nbsp;Adolfo Henriques. This 162(m) compensation primarily relates to sign-on grants
provided to Mr.&nbsp;Henriques as detailed in his employment agreement.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">During 2004, the U.S. Congress passed the American Jobs Creation Act of 2004, which included
certain tax law changes that effect taxable earnings (starting in 2005) of FECI. Generally, these
tax law changes are expected to benefit the Company through additional current period tax credits
for rail right-of-way maintenance expenses (previously capitalized and depreciated) and reduced
federal taxes on diesel fuel, respectively. The tax credit for track maintenance is limited
(annually)&nbsp;to $3,500 per mile of track and available for years 2005 through 2007. Between December
31, 2004 and June&nbsp;30, 2005 the railroad fuel tax was reduced from $0.043 per gallon to $0.033 per
gallon. From July&nbsp;1, 2005 to December&nbsp;31, 2006, the tax will be reduced to $0.023 per gallon.
From January&nbsp;1, 2007, the tax is eliminated.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Net Income</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">As a result of the foregoing factors, net income was $49.4&nbsp;million, or $1.52 per diluted share, for
2005, compared to $80.6&nbsp;million, or $2.29 per diluted share, for 2004 and $43.2&nbsp;million, or $1.17
per diluted share in 2003.
</DIV>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 12pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>OTHER MATTERS</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 12pt; margin-top: 6pt"><U><B>Critical Accounting Policies</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The Company has reviewed its accounting policies to identify those that are very important to the
portrayal of the Company&#146;s financial condition and results and that require management&#146;s most
difficult, subjective or complex judgments (the Critical Accounting Policies). The Company has
determined that its accounting policies governing impairment of long-lived assets, income tax
provisions, realty rental revenue recognition, real estate infrastructure costs and contingencies
may be considered Critical Accounting Policies.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><U>Impairment of Long-lived Assets</U> &#150; The Company reviews the carrying value of its property,
plant and equipment for impairment whenever events or changes in circumstances indicate the
carrying value of an asset or asset grouping may not be recoverable from the undiscounted future
net cash flows expected to be generated over its remaining useful life. Detecting these events or
changes in circumstances can be difficult. At December&nbsp;31, 2005, management had not identified
indicators of an impairment of value for any significant asset group of the Company. However,
events or changes in circumstances could develop or occur that would cause the Company to evaluate
the recovery of its long-lived assets and potentially record an impairment, the amount of which
could be material. Assets that are deemed impaired are recorded at the lower of carrying amount or
fair value.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><U>Income Tax Provisions</U> &#151; FECI&#146;s net deferred tax liability was $185.0&nbsp;million at December
31, 2005. At December&nbsp;31, 2005, the Company had a state deferred tax asset of approximately $4.6
million relating to net operating loss carry forwards. The net deferred tax liability is estimated
based on the expected future tax consequences of items recognized in the financial statements.
Management must use judgment when estimating the future timing and deductibility of expenses and
loss and credit carry forwards in the Company&#146;s tax returns. A valuation allowance is required to
be recorded if management expects that it be more likely than not that its deferred tax assets will
not be realized. As of December&nbsp;31, 2005, FECI has no valuation allowance since it expects to
realize its deferred tax assets. This expectation is primarily based upon FECI&#146;s expectation that
future operations will be sufficiently profitable to utilize the operating loss carry forwards, as
well as the existence of various tax, business and other planning strategies available to the
Company. However, the Company cannot guarantee that it will realize this tax asset or that future
valuation allowances will not be required. Failure to utilize the tax asset could materially
affect the Company&#146;s financial results and financial position. Federal tax years through 1999 have
been closed with the Internal Revenue Service (IRS). Federal tax years 2000-2002 are currently
under review by the IRS. Management believes its income tax provision reserves at December&nbsp;31,
2005 to be adequate for the settlement, if any, of disputed tax positions taken by the Company for
these years. Historically, settlements for disputed tax positions that have effected the total tax
provision provided for in the income statement has been primarily interest charges on timing
differences between years.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><U>Revenue Recognition &#150; Realty Rental Revenues</U> &#151; Revenues from realty rentals are primarily
contractual base rents from leases of commercial property. The Company recognizes revenues from
these base rents evenly over the lease term in accordance with SFAS 13, &#147;Accounting for Leases&#148;
(SFAS 13). SFAS 13 requires rental income from an operating lease be recognized on a straight-line
basis over the non-cancelable lease term. Accordingly, total contractual minimum lease payments,
including scheduled rent increases, are recognized as rental revenue evenly over the lease term.
Accrued revenues from contractually scheduled rent increases in excess of amounts currently due
amounted to $22.1&nbsp;million at December&nbsp;31, 2005, which is reported in current and long-term assets.
At December&nbsp;31, 2005, there are two leases that each total more the 10% of this amount. The
combined total for these two tenants is $6.5&nbsp;million. The total for the top five leases is $9.1
million. The Company monitors this asset for collection risk and establishes reserves for any
amounts deemed not collectible. However, amounts collected in future periods may vary from the
Company&#146;s expectations.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><U>Real Estate Infrastructure Costs</U> &#150; The Company periodically constructs road, road
structures and general infrastructure related to the development of commercial real estate.
Certain of these infrastructure assets may be deeded, upon completion, to applicable governmental
authorities. The Company believes these deeded items (e.g., roads) benefit and increase the value
of the parcels within and adjacent to the related commercial real estate developments. The Company
capitalizes all infrastructure costs (including costs of infrastructure assets deeded to
governmental authorities) into the basis of its commercial real estate developments, and allocates
these costs to the individual parcels within the park on a relative fair value basis in accordance
with SFAS No.&nbsp;67, &#147;Accounting for Costs and Initial Rental Operations of Real Estate Projects&#148;
(SFAS 67).
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">In connection with infrastructure assets constructed by the Company and deeded to governmental
authorities, certain governmental authorities may agree to reimburse the Company certain of its
construction costs for the deeded assets based on and to the extent of annual incremental taxes
(such as property taxes) as may be collected by the governmental authority from the applicable
properties over a stipulated period of time. Prior to and at the date of deeding the
infrastructure asset to the governmental authority, any current reimbursements were accounted for
as a reduction in the allocated costs described above to the extent the Company&#146;s receipt of such
reimbursements had become fixed and determinable, based upon the related
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">39
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">incremental taxes having been assessed and being, therefore, payable. The Company does not
recognize expected future tax reimbursements even though they may be probable and estimable, until
they become fixed and determinable.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Under a current agreement with the City of Jacksonville and the Florida Department of
Transportation (FDOT), Flagler constructed and deeded to the FDOT an interchange asset in the third
quarter of 2004. During 2004, prior to completion of the interchange asset, Flagler received $2.1
million that was applied as a reduction in the basis of the asset. In June&nbsp;2005, Flagler received
a reimbursement from the City of Jacksonville for $0.7&nbsp;million, of which $0.5&nbsp;million was applied
against depreciation expense on infrastructure assets. The remaining $0.2&nbsp;million of the
reimbursement was recorded as other non-operating income. This reimbursement received after the
completion of the infrastructure asset was allocated between operating and non-operating activities
based on the relative assessed value of property within Flagler Center, owned by Flagler versus
that sold to outside parties. Future reimbursements will be treated similarly.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><U>Contingencies</U> &#150; It is the Company&#146;s policy to reserve for certain contingencies consisting
primarily of various claims and lawsuits resulting from its operations. In the opinion of
management, appropriate reserves have been made for the estimated liability that may result from
disposition of such matters. Management&#146;s opinion and ultimately the reserve recorded in the
financial statements are based on known facts and circumstances. In certain circumstances,
management uses the services of outside counsel to help evaluate the facts and circumstances and
considers their professional judgment in establishing the appropriate reserve. The ultimate
resolution of these contingencies may be for an amount greater or less than the amount estimated by
management.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Beginning in 2004, the Company has sought recovery of costs it incurred to repair or operate
automatic warning devices at grade crossings during periods of power outages caused by hurricanes.
The Company was reimbursed $1.0&nbsp;million from state and local governments for hurricanes occurring
in 2004; in 2005, the Company billed $0.9&nbsp;million for similar reimbursements.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Trading Activities that Include Non-Exchange Traded Contracts Accounted for at Fair Value</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The Company does not participate in trading of non-exchange traded contracts accounted for at fair
value.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Effects of Transactions with Related and Certain Other Parties</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The Company does not have transactions with related and certain other parties other than those
transactions that may be described in Item&nbsp;13. Certain Relationships and Related Transactions.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Environmental Liabilities</B></U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The Company is subject to proceedings arising out of environmental laws and regulations, which
primarily relate to the disposal and use of fuel and oil used in the transportation business. It
is the Company&#146;s policy to accrue and charge against earnings environmental cleanup costs when it
is probable that a liability has been incurred and an amount can be reasonably estimated. As
assessments and cleanups proceed, these accruals are reviewed and adjusted.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Compliance with the federal, state, and local laws and regulations relating to the protection of
the environment has not affected the Company&#146;s capital additions, earnings or competitive position,
nor does management anticipate any future matters will adversely affect the Company&#146;s financial
situation based upon the information available today.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Environmental expenditures for capital improvements and ongoing operations and maintenance have
historically been insignificant to the operations of the Company. Management does not anticipate
any material changes in these expenditures. These expenditures are expected to be funded from
current operations.
</DIV>

</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 12pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>ITEM 7A. </B><B><I>QUANTITATIVE AND QUALITATIVE DISCLOSURE ABOUT MARKET RISK</I></B>
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The Company&#146;s primary market risk exposure has historically been interest rate risk primarily
related to the Company&#146;s outstanding debt, as further discussed in Note 15 to the financial
statements continued in Item&nbsp;8 of this Form 10-K.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">On February&nbsp;22, 2005, the Company entered into a new unsecured $150&nbsp;million, 5-year revolving
credit agreement with a syndication of banks. The new credit facility provides a more favorable
interest rate and terms and replaces the Company&#146;s previous $200&nbsp;million credit agreement. The
Company pays (quarterly)&nbsp;commitment fees, as applicable under the agreement, at a range of 12.5 to
25.0 basis points. The Company&#146;s revolving credit agreement requires the maintenance of certain
financial ratios (interest coverage and leverage) and maintenance of minimum established levels of
net worth. The Company believes the most restrictive of such ratios is the Leverage Ratio (as
defined in the Credit Facility Agreement). The Credit Facility Agreement provides that at December
31, 2005, the Company&#146;s Adjusted Total Debt/Adjusted Total EBITDA ratio shall be no greater than
3.00 to 1.00. At December&nbsp;31, 2005, the Company&#146;s actual Adjusted Total Debt/Adjusted Total EBITDA
ratio was 0.02 to 1.00. In addition there are various established limitations (dollars and
otherwise) on certain types of liens, investments (including loans and advances) and acquisitions,
limitation on transactions with affiliates, merger and sales of all or substantially all of the
assets; and use of proceeds. Some of the above covenants provide specific exclusion of certain
financing and investing activities at Flagler. Borrowings under the credit agreement bear interest
at variable rates linked to the LIBOR Index. Interest on borrowings is due and payable on the
&#147;rollover date&#148; for each draw. Outstanding borrowings can be paid at any time by the borrower, or
at the conclusion of the facility&#146;s term. At December&nbsp;31, 2005, there were no borrowings
outstanding on this facility.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The following table presents the future principal cash flows and weighted-average interest rates
expected on the Company&#146;s existing mortgage notes. Fair value has been determined based on current
rates available to the Company at December&nbsp;31, 2005 for similar maturity instruments.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 12pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>ITEM 8. </B><B><I>FINANCIAL STATEMENTS AND SUPPLEMENTARY DATA</I></B>
</DIV>

<DIV align="left">
<A name="305"></A>
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Report of Independent Registered Public Accounting Firm

</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The Board of Directors and Shareholders<BR>
Florida East Coast Industries, Inc.

</DIV>
<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">We have audited the accompanying consolidated balance sheets of Florida East Coast Industries, Inc.
and subsidiaries as of December&nbsp;31, 2005 and 2004, and the related consolidated statements of
income, changes in shareholders&#146; equity and comprehensive income, and cash flows for each of the
years in the three-year period ended December&nbsp;31, 2005. In connection with our audits of the
consolidated financial statements, we also audited the financial statement schedules II and III as
listed in the accompanying Index on Page 71 of this Report on Form 10-K for the year 2005. These
consolidated financial statements and financial statement schedules are the responsibility of the
Company&#146;s management. Our responsibility is to express an opinion on these consolidated financial
statements and financial statement schedules based on our audits.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">We conducted our audits in accordance with the standards of the Public Company Accounting Oversight
Board (United States). Those standards require that we plan and perform the audit to obtain
reasonable assurance about whether the financial statements are free of material misstatement. An
audit includes examining, on a test basis, evidence supporting the amounts and disclosures in the
financial statements. An audit also includes assessing the accounting principles used and
significant estimates made by management, as well as evaluating the overall financial statement
presentation. We believe that our audits provide a reasonable basis for our opinion.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">In our opinion, the consolidated financial statements referred to above present fairly, in all
material respects, the financial position of Florida East Coast Industries, Inc. and subsidiaries
as of December&nbsp;31, 2005 and 2004, and the results of their operations and their cash flows for each
of the years in the three-year period ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, in conformity with U. S. generally
accepted accounting principles. Also, in our opinion, the related financial statement schedules,
when considered in relation to the basic consolidated financial statements taken as a whole,
present fairly, in all material respects, the information set forth therein.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">We also have audited, in accordance with the standards of the Public Company Accounting Oversight
Board (United States), the effectiveness of Florida East Coast Industries, Inc. and subsidiaries,
internal control over financial reporting as of December&nbsp;31, 2005, based on criteria established in
Internal Control &#150; Integrated Framework issued by the Committee of Sponsoring Organizations of the
Treadway Commission (COSO), and our report dated February&nbsp;28, 2006 expressed an unqualified opinion
on management&#146;s assessment of, and the effective operation of, internal control over financial
reporting.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">/s/ KPMG LLP

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U><B>Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements</B></U>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 0pt"><B>Years Ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, 2004 and 2003</B>

</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Note 1. Nature of Business</B>
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The principal operations of Florida East Coast Industries, Inc. (the Company or FECI) and its
subsidiaries primarily relate to the transportation of goods by rail and the acquisition,
development, leasing, management and sale of real estate. The Company&#146;s rail subsidiary, Florida
East Coast Railway, LLC (FECR or Railway), operates primarily within the state of Florida, with
approximately 60% of its revenues derived from local moves along the Company&#146;s line. Approximately
42% of the freight revenues were derived from five of its largest customers, with approximately
$45.3&nbsp;million, $38.7&nbsp;million, and $34.7&nbsp;million, 20%, 21%, and 20%, in 2005, 2004 and 2003,
respectively, generated by one significant customer. The Company&#146;s realty subsidiary, Flagler
Development Company (Flagler), presently operates in Florida with business parks in Jacksonville,
Orlando and the South Florida markets. Flagler&#146;s operating properties are Class &#147;A&#148; office space
and high-quality commercial/industrial facilities.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Note
2. Subsequent Event &#151; Acquisition of Codina Group and Related Interests</B>
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">On January&nbsp;6, 2006, the Company announced that a newly formed holding company and certain Company
subsidiaries had entered into various agreements to acquire privately held Codina Group, Inc. and
certain related property interests held directly or indirectly by Mr.&nbsp;Armando Codina for total
consideration of $270&nbsp;million. The total consideration of $270&nbsp;million includes $66&nbsp;million of
cash for certain property interests, assumption or repayment of $36&nbsp;million of debt and up to $168
million of equity securities (includes up to $85&nbsp;million of contingent consideration). The
contingent equity is contingent upon achievement of certain value based milestones including $30
million tied to certain land use approvals, $30&nbsp;million upon certain partnership distributions from
a joint venture, $20&nbsp;million is based on cumulative FECI stock price improvements of up to an
additional $27.00 over the next five years; and $5&nbsp;million is dependent on the new holding company
agreeing to move forward with a proposed residential development. The purchase price is subject to
post-closing adjustment based on certain closing conditions. The Codina acquisition is expected to
close during the first quarter of 2006 subject to certain closing conditions.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Headquartered in Coral Gables, Florida, Codina Group, Inc. is an integrated commercial real estate,
development, construction, leasing, property management and brokerage firm with 150 employees. To
date, the Codina Group has constructed over 11&nbsp;million sq. ft. of industrial, office and retail
property, developed over 1,760 acres of land, and currently manages more than 12&nbsp;million sq. ft. of
property.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">In addition to Codina Group, the transactions will include the following projects under current and
future development in South Florida:
</DIV>
